The new wildfire reality

0:00 · 80% of wildfire destruction is caused by one type of fire that until recently was rare. Scientists just figured out what these destructive fires have in common, and it’s not what you might guess to understand what’s going on. We talked to Battalion Chief, Mike McClintock, who fought this type of fire during the summer of 2024.

0:21 · And our conventional methods with fire engines and personnel were really ineffective. We had to get ahead of the fire and worry about life safety To understand why wildfire destruction has increased. We focus on the most obvious change in fires: they’re getting bigger, but researcher Jennifer Balch says that might be a distraction. According to her new study, We’ve been focused on the wrong metric.

0:43 · The number of homes burned each year is going up. The Rim fire in California was massive at over 257,000 acres, but it only destroyed 11 homes and no one died. When you compare that to the Lahaina fire in Maui, which was a hundred times smaller, burned 2200 homes and buildings and was the deadliest fire in modern U.S. history, it’s obvious that we’re missing something.

Revisiting fire research

1:09 · In this episode, we’re going to look at new research that could fundamentally change how we think about wildfire risk, what to expect in the future and what we can do to prevent losses. With entire towns being wiped off the map like Paradise, California, Jennifer and her team wanted to understand why.

1:32 · To figure out solutions, he knew that the first step was properly defining the problem, so rather than thinking about size, she approached fire from a new angle.

1:42 · What do the most destructive fires have in common?

1:45 · And until recently she didn’t have the tools to answer this question.

1:49 · We’ve had really good data on how big they are, but we’re only getting to the point where the satellites are getting good enough to give us information on a much finer temporal scale on an hour by hour basis.

1:59 · We have new high resolution data because NASA is doing a lot more than putting astronauts in space these days.

2:06 · NASA’s done a lot over the years with studying the earth. We develop technology. We eventually launch into space on satellites, and we can see processes unfold across really large scales that we often can’t see from a ground-based or human perspective.

2:17 · Jennifer looked at the time-based images of 60,000 fire events in the U.S. to analyze the way they spread and suddenly it made sense.

2:26 · This is a new discovery that we have made by putting together all of these different data sources and looking at fire from space.

Speed dictates destruction

2:33 · Today, so many science discoveries don’t happen in the field, but in simulations powered by enormous supercomputers that perform quadrillions of calculations each second to model our climate. I got to visit one of these supercomputers in my new show, Weathered: Earth’s Extremes, where I traveled the world to find out where we are and where we’re going in this moment of climate change. You can watch right now on the PBS app, but back to fire.

2:58 · The results of Jennifer’s investigation were striking. What almost all destructive fires have in common between 2001 and 2020 is speed. And this finding fundamentally changes how we approach fire management.

3:12 · Fires that move that fast.

3:13 · More than 4,000 acres a day account for 88% of the fire related damages that we see across the U.S. They are only 3% of the fires on record during that time. A very small number of fire events have huge consequences.

3:28 · And fast fires themselves changed in a worrying way during the study window. We’ll get to that in a bit, but first, why are fast fires so destructive?

Firefighting in fast fires

3:37 · And why are they so destructive in a place like Southern California with some of the most experienced firefighters in the world, backed by impressive air support?

3:46 · The Bridge Fire was a great example, the fire activity was so significant, our conventional methods with fire engines and personnel were really ineffective.

3:54 · When fires are moving this fast, it’s virtually impossible to put them out. When Mike’s crew deployed to the Mountain High Ski Resort, wind was blowing embers miles ahead of the flaming front. Smoke reduced visibility and grounded aircrafts.

4:08 · Winds were significant at more than 50 miles an hour, we realized that the fire suppression, putting this fire out, on our side is not gonna happen and we’re gonna have to move and shift our priorities.

4:19 · The fire was spreading quickly towards the town of Wrightwood just three miles away.

4:24 · There’s a lot of similarities between the community of Paradise and the community of Wrightwood. We knew that going up there, we were gonna have a heck of a firefight.

4:33 · The comparison to Paradise, California is significant.

4:36 · In 2018, another fast fire burned over 18,000 structures and homes and killed almost 90 people. So the crew raced in an attempt to reach Wrightwood before the fire did.

4:48 · We had, you know, upwards of 50 engines on their way or there. We still had over 3000 structures immediately threatened.

4:56 · If you were to ask me at 30 minutes into this incident, where was our structure loss gonna be?

5:01 · I would say probably in upwards of one to 200.

5:04 · So they fought from house to house and help residents evacuate. By the end of September 10th, the Bridge Fire had exploded from 4,000 acres to over 34,000. This fire is part of a growing trend. During Jennifer’s 20 year study period, fires got 249% faster across the West and 400% faster in California.

Drivers of rapid spread

5:26 · But what’s making them faster?

5:29 · Forest management is often regarded as a primary driver of fire behavior. But according to Jennifer, it isn’t a significant factor in fast fires.

5:38 · The fastest fires are actually in grasslands.

5:40 · They’re very flammable fuels. They’re very thin, they’re easy to catch on fire. They host fires that move really fast.

5:46 · In fact, 80% of fast fires in Jennifer’s study occurred primarily in grass and shrublands, not forests. It turns out that there are three elements needed for fast fire. One: ignition, two: dry, receptive fuel, and three: wind. Wind and ignition are difficult to change and control, but there’s a piece of the fast fire puzzle that’s a key force in their rise: dry, receptive fuel.

6:14 · We’ve had fuels that have been drying out all summer long and then we just throw in a little weather and a fire. And those are mixtures and that recipe for extreme fire behavior.

6:23 · And firefighters even have a term for this fire weather. In Canada, they call it crossover because it occurs when the temperature in Celsius reaches and crosses over the relative humidity level and it’s explosive. At that point, fire can rip through dry fuels and spread extremely fast. In the U.S. we use the more general term red flag conditions.

6:46 · So what does that mean for the future of fast fires?

Climate change and risk

6:50 · With increasing temperatures, we know that we’re essentially pulling more moisture out of fuels, we’re making them drier. And that’s the big impact of climate change on fire is making fuels more available and more available throughout the year.

7:05 · We’re seeing extreme weather patterns change and and intensify across the globe. We’re getting all sorts of situations in which weather patterns are driving extreme fire behavior.

7:13 · Models predict that the number of extreme fire spread events will more than double with two degrees Celsius of global warming, plus a doubling of acres burned. And that’s not far from our current level of warming. We can’t have a fast fire without strong wind. But the type of wind that causes many destructive fires has a special characteristic that makes it particularly dangerous.

The danger of wind events

7:35 · We worked with NASA to create scientifically accurate animations of the wind and fire progressions during a handful of fast, destructive fires to illustrate what’s going on.

7:45 · I’m hard pressed to think of an event that destroyed more than a hundred homes that didn’t have wind involved.

7:51 · And the common denominator for us to make it hard to control wildfires is wind.

7:55 · When strong winds meet or start a wildfire, they throw embers miles ahead of the flaming front, starting new fires and spreading the wildfire outward. These conditions supercharged the East Troublesome Fire. The fire was so fast, moving at 18 miles in a single day, that it jumped The Continental Divide, an area with very little fuel for fire. In the Marshall Fire, we can see a type of wind that’s particularly dangerous: down slope winds.

8:23 · As wind drops in elevation, atmospheric pressure increases, which dries and warms the air. Temperatures can increase by as much as 5.5 degrees Fahrenheit for every 1000 feet of lost elevation, drying vegetation, and eliminating the need for high temperatures with wildfire. The fire started on December 30th, just outside of Boulder, Colorado.

8:46 · Wind gusts of up to 115 miles per hour flowed off the front range of the Rocky Mountains, sucking any remaining moisture out of the vegetation to create the most destructive fire in state history. Destroying over a thousand homes in six hours. In Lahaina, wind blew down the mountain, drying vegetation, warming the air, and knocking down power lines that ignited grasses, then houses, destroying much of the town.

9:13 · During the Labor Day fires, river canyons turned into wind tunnels with speeds reaching 100 miles per hour. Wind blew over power lines and ignited fires in and around small communities, fanning existing fires to create one of the most destructive fire seasons in state history. These aren’t just regular wildfires, they’re wind events with fire in them.

Preparing for future fires

9:36 · In those situations where you have an extreme wind event, basically the fire becomes uncontrollable. You know, fire suppression efforts are extremely ineffective in that situation. ’cause basically, for safety reasons, the firefighters just have to get out of the way. And so there’s, there’s really no stopping it.

9:50 · And that’s probably the most important lesson about fast fires. Once they’ve started, there’s no stopping them until the wind dies down. Firefighters can only evacuate people and wait. And as we face a hotter, drier, future fire prone places are going to become far more challenging places to live.

10:08 · So how do we reduce the destruction?

10:11 · We prepare ahead of time. Let’s go back to the Bridge Fire, where Mike expected hundreds of houses to be lost.

10:19 · I feel like the Bridge Fire in Wrightwood is a success story.

10:21 · The Wrightwood community did everything right to prepare their homes for fire. The result? Only about 10 homes burned rather than one to 200 that Mike expected.

10:31 · So how do we prepare our homes for fire?

10:34 · To answer this question, I ask the Chief of all fire chiefs.

10:37 · Understanding how to live with the fire that’s inevitable is absolutely key to our survival of the communities across the Nation.

10:45 · The research shows that we can significantly reduce the chance that homes will ignite in fast fires by taking simple steps focused on our home and its immediate surroundings.

10:56 · So it is incumbent on community planners, on individuals who purchase the properties on builders that they also build to code. Codes that are based on science: fire resistant materials, proper roofing materials, proper windows, not having wood fences. These are key pieces of the puzzle here.

11:16 · If we’re going to continue to build communities in the path of fire, then we must build them according to the science and the codes to make people saveable in their own homes, in their own communities.

11:28 · If you wanna learn more about the ways you can prepare your home for fire, tornadoes, or even hurricanes, check out our new series Weathered: Earth’s Extremes by downloading the PBS app today.

11:43 · Have you survived a fire yourself?

11:45 · What are you doing to prepare your home?

Congestion pricing works – just look at London.

New York Gov. Andrew Cuomo recently announced a plan to bring congestion pricing to New York City. The goal is to raise money for the city’s crumbling public transit system and reclaim the dangerously busy city streets. But what is congestion pricing, and can it actually solve all our transit woes?

We took a look at London, a city that enacted a congestion charge in 2003, to see some of the benefits. Check out the video above to learn more.

The problem with urban traffic

0:00 · So, it’s 5 o’clock in New York City and I’m about to catch a cab about 5 miles uptown.

0:07 · Let’s see how this goes.

0:24 · So it just took me 40 minutes to go about 4.5 miles which is pretty typical for New York City rush hour.

0:32 · Despite a speed limit of 25 miles per hour, the average car moving through NYC is driving at just 7.1 MPH, down from 9.1 MPH in 2010.

0:42 · And if you’re in midtown it’s even worse, with cars moving around 5 MPH.

0:47 · But it’s not just New York City – traffic in cities like LA is so bad drivers could be locked in gridlock for hours.

0:54 · Of course this sucks for drivers, but it also makes activities like biking or walking less safe because cyclists and pedestrians have to weave through an obstacle course of cars.

1:04 · Not to mention the estimated 20 billion dollars in lost revenue due to wasted time sitting in traffic.

1:10 · Now, there might be a solution, but if you commute by car, you are probably not gonna like it.

Introducing congestion pricing

1:17 · It’s called congestion pricing. And it means charging drivers for using the roads.

1:21 · “Congestion pricing is an idea whose time has come. And I believe this is the year to actually get it done.”

1:31 · New York’s plan is still in the works, and it probably won’t be enacted until 2020.

1:36 · But the end game is to reduce congestion by discouraging people from driving if they have other options like biking, or taking a train, or walking.

1:44 · And to fund public transit at the same time.

1:48 · It’s not a groundbreaking idea: congestion pricing is already old news in cities around the world.

The London model

1:53 · London enacted a similar policy in 2003.

1:56 · This is a necessary step for us to reclaim some of the space that is currently given to a motorized vehicles without ending up with gridlock.

2:04 · Nicole Badstuber researches urban infrastructure and policy at the University of Cambridge and according to her, the system’s pretty simple.

2:12 · When drivers enter the Central London congestion zone between 7 a.m. and 6 p.m., they’re charged 11 pounds 50 pence – about 15 US dollars.

2:21 · New York City’s plan will be similar.

2:22 · When drivers enter Midtown or Lower Manhattan, they’ll face a fee.

2:26 · There’s cameras all around the roads at the edges of the congestion charging area.

2:31 · They automatically recognize the name plate of the car or the vehicle entering the zone.

2:36 · London has a few exemptions in place, like for people who live inside the congestion zone or vehicles with 9 or more seats and New York City will likely do that, too. And the system works.

2:47 · So since it was introduced, we’ve seen that private vehicles entering the zone have gone down by 40 percent.

2:54 · Overall vehicle traffic has gone down by 25 percent.

Results and public space

2:57 · Cycling overall has increased 66% since the charge was instituted and bus ridership reached a 50-year high in 2011.

3:04 · And wait times for buses decreased 25%, due to increased service both on buses and on the London Underground.

3:10 · So we now, in comparison, still have much higher frequencies of London Underground services.

3:15 · We can get more people, more capacity, more people into our trains because we have newer trains.

3:20 · And like Nicole said, congestion pricing isn’t just about removing cars from specific zones, it’s about reclaiming a space for the public.

3:28 · Picture Trafalgar Square, but designed for cars – an idea that was very much a reality before congestion pricing.

3:35 · You would basically have a bus driving right past your nose as you come out of the National Gallery Reclaiming that section of road made the square safer and opened it to more public events.

3:44 · No one could imagine going back to what it was before, and having these cars and buses zoom past you.

Resistance and future goals

3:52 · London’s plan is widely embraced today, but it was met with resistance at first, with opponents arguing that congestion pricing could cut people off from health care, shopping, and schools.

4:02 · Plus, people had to trust that the government would work efficiently and make significant improvements to their public transit system.

4:09 · But within a year, London’s congestion charging had majority support.

4:13 · As New York’s plan is being finalized, some similar resistance is cropping up, which isn’t too surprising.

4:19 · After all, it’s the first US city to implement this type of congestion pricing and no one wants to pay for something they’ve gotten for free for so long.

4:27 · But the plan could generate up to a billion dollars for public transit, a system that most agree desperately needs repair.

4:33 · And the city estimates it will reduce congestion by 8 to 13 percent and increase speeds by up to 9 percent, making a ride through midtown a lot easier.

4:43 · So, like other cities where congestion pricing has been successful, it’s likely that people will end up accepting it.

Reimagining public roads

4:49 · When we think of our roads, in particular in cities, as a sort of public good, as a public space, then if you’re taking up more of it you should probably be paying for that privilege.

5:01 · If you start to think about how everyone gets around the city, charging cars begins to make a lot more sense: You pay for parking, pay for the subway, pay to take a train or a bus, so why wouldn’t we pay for a city road?

There’s Something Living in Wildfire Smoke

NOVA PBS Official, 01 13, 2025

Video (1:58)
Description

Wildfire smoke can carry thousands of living microorganisms, like bacteria and fungi, across long distances. Some of them could potentially infect humans. With wildfires on the rise, experts are studying the risk this poses to our health.

📰 Stay up to date on the latest science discoveries, full episodes, articles, videos, and more by signing up for NOVA’s newsletter here: https://www.pbs.org/wgbh/nova/newsletter/

🛍️ Shop official NOVA 50th anniversary merch: https://nova-shop.org/

TikTok: https://www.tiktok.com/@novapbs

Transcript

0:01 · [Narrator] Wildfire smoke is loaded with harmful particles like charred plant matter, incinerated materials, and noxious gases. But it’s also carrying stuff that’s alive, thousands of teeny-tiny microorganisms like bacteria and fungi.

0:15 · But why doesn’t the heat from a wildfire kill all these microorganisms?

0:19 · Well, not all parts of a wildfire are hot.

0:23 · When we look at fire on the scale of a microorganism, there are likely pockets of cold air, areas that are not being burned at all.

0:32 · [Narrator] In those tiny pockets of cooler air, microorganisms can catch rides on bits of carbon, or water vapor, rise high into the atmosphere and travel long distances. And some could potentially infect humans. Some health experts are already noticing a concerning relationship between wildfire smoke exposure and rates of infection. One study in California found an uptick in fungal infections after wildfires within a 200-mile radius of hospitals. And climate change is making wildfires more intense and more impactful.

1:08 · Compared to the yearly average in the 1970s, the average wildfire season in the US is now 105 days longer, burns six times the number of acres and has three times as many large fires.

1:21 · Fires are affected by dry conditions and warmer conditions. Climate change is causing more of those extremes. More extreme conditions, more extreme wildfires. They go together, hand by hand.

1:34 · [Narrator] Though there are still many unknowns, risk is growing for more people.

1:40 · We would expect that we’re all just gonna become exposed on a regular basis. We’d have to look out for that in places that we wouldn’t normally be on the lookout. (gentle music)

What is Climate Change

CrashCourse, 08 15, 2023

Video (13:57)
Description

Life on Earth has weathered boiling-hot oceans and volcanic-ash-darkened skies—but that’s nothing like the climate change we’re experiencing now. In this episode of Crash Course Biology, we’ll talk about the greenhouse effect, learn why our climate is like a tangled pair of headphones, and discover that we’ve understood the science behind climate change for much longer than you might think.

Transcript
Introduction to Climate Change

0:00 · Our planet has been draped in ice sheets, filled with boiling-hot oceans, and dimmed by volcanic ash – all before anything more complicated than a single-celled organism showed up.

0:10 · So climate change is nothing new around here… when it happens gradually, over millions of years.

0:17 · But something new has happened in the last few centuries.

0:20 · People like us…except, wearing hats like this… began burning fossil fuels like coal, oil, and natural gas to make energy.

0:29 · Most of us aren’t wearing hats like those anymore.

0:31 · But we’re still powering our daily lives and industries with those fuels, releasing billions of tons of carbon dioxide every year.

0:41 · And that’s caused Earth’s climate to change in the span of just a few human lifetimes, the geological blink of an eye.

0:48 · Hi! I’m Dr. Sammy, your friendly neighborhood entomologist, and this is Crash Course Biology!

0:56 · Hey, do you guys smell that?

0:58 · …smells like…

1:00 · theme music?

1:01 · [THEME MUSIC] Now, I know what you’re thinking.

1:12 · “Wait, isn’t this Crash Course Biology?”

1:15 · And yeah, this episode is heavy on the gases, low on the golgi bodies.

1:19 · And that might leave you wondering, what does climate change have to do with the science of life?

1:25 · Here’s the thing: life and climate are tied together, like that tangled pair of headphones at the bottom of your backpack.

1:30 · You know, like you tug one end and then a knot tightens, which is looped around a paperclip, somehow snagged on that tiny notebook where you drew hearts around your crush’s name, which after several tugs is now lying open on the floor and…I’m breaking into a cold sweat just thinking about it.

1:46 · But back to the point, we can’t talk about life without talking about climate —which doesn’t mean last week’s thunderstorm or a one-day temperature swing – that’s weather.

1:58 · Climate is long-term weather conditions averaged over many years.

2:03 · To understand the difference between them, just remember that knowing the weather will help you decide if you should grab an umbrella before you head out, but knowing the climate will help you decide if you should invest in a good air conditioner.

2:15 · While the weather might impact your choice of clothing for the day, the climate directly impacts where and when different kinds of life can survive.

The Greenhouse Effect

2:24 · We owe today’s climate to the fact that our little green-and-blue marble of a home isn’t just floating in space unprotected.

2:31 · It’s wrapped up in an atmosphere; a big, invisible, gassy jacket.

2:35 · Which, granted, sounds pretty weird when you put it that way.

2:38 · But without it, we wouldn’t exist.

2:40 · This jacket is made of different kinds of gases.

2:43 · And a small fraction of them, known as greenhouse gases, absorb solar energy like, super well.

2:49 · We’re talking about gases like methane, water vapor, and most importantly carbon dioxide – also known as CO2.

2:58 · They account for less than half of one percent of our atmospheric jacket.

3:03 · But they’re a part of what makes it so good at trapping heat, sort of like all the little white feathers in your puffy coat.

3:09 · When sunlight beams down from space, most of it travels through those gases with no problem.

3:14 · The energy from that sunlight gets absorbed as it strikes the Earth, warming the surface.

3:19 · That type of warming is normal and seasonal.

3:22 · The Earth naturally bounces some of that solar energy back toward space.

3:27 · Where some of it exits the atmosphere and heads right back out into the inky ether.

3:32 · But the rest of that energy gets trapped by the Earth’s gassy jacket.

3:36 · Specifically by those super absorbent greenhouse gases, which suck up heat and bounce it back down to us again.

3:44 · This warming process is called the greenhouse effect.

3:47 · Unsurprisingly, It works the same way as a greenhouse – using layers of glass to trap heat inside.

3:54 · And it keeps Earth at a nice, cozy, insulated average of 14 degrees Celsius.

4:00 · Without it, our Earth would be a chilly average of -18 degrees Celsius.

4:06 · Great for storing ice cream!

4:08 · Not so good for rainforests, swimsuits, or us for that matter.

4:13 · So, the greenhouse effect is a natural, helpful process that makes Earth habitable for all of life!

4:18 · But you can have too much of a good thing.

4:21 · When our atmospheric jacket contains more carbon dioxide, for example, it gets really good at trapping heat.

4:28 · Like, too good.

4:30 · And the hotter things get, the more water evaporates and joins the atmosphere— and remember, water vapor itself is a greenhouse gas, so that in turn absorbs even more heat, creating a looping system of cause-and-effect that just keeps reinforcing itself.

Measuring the Greenhouse Effect

4:46 · And while dressing in layers is great if you’re hiking in the Alps, it’s really hard for our planet to shed its extra coats.

4:52 · So all of that heat gets stuck going from the ground to the atmosphere like the worst game of hot potato ever played.

4:59 · When I learned about this, I was like, “Wow, what a revelation!

5:03 · I can’t believe we’ve only recently figured this out!”

5:05 · But it turns out that this isn’t new knowledge; we’ve known how, and why, this could happen for nearly 170 years.

5:16 · Let’s pay a visit to the Theater of Life… [Inquisitive music] Back in 1856, Eunice Foote, an American scientist and suffragette, was thinking about how the Sun’s warmth affected different gases.

5:27 · In those days, the scientific community was a bit like a fort with a handmade “no girls allowed” sign out front.

5:34 · But Foote wasn’t deterred, and ran her experiments anyway.

5:38 · She filled tubes with different combinations of gases, including carbon dioxide.

5:42 · After putting some tubes in the Sun and some in the shade, she compared their temperatures, trying to find the hottest gas.

5:50 · All the tubes in direct sunlight warmed up.

5:52 · But none as intensely as the tube that contained carbon dioxide.

5:57 · The temperature had soared to 51.7 degrees Celsius, hot enough to burn your fingers.

6:04 · From that insight, Foote theorized that if our atmosphere ever contained more carbon dioxide, the whole planet would warm up as a result, which would mean a lot more than a few scalded fingers.

6:16 · And sure enough, today, that’s exactly the situation we’re in.

6:20 · Foote was one of the first scientists to recognize carbon dioxide’s potential to affect Earth’s climate.

6:25 · But she wasn’t the only one to connect the dots.

6:28 · For example, just a few decades later, another scientist named Dr. Svante Arrhenius observed that burning coal releases carbon dioxide.

6:37 · As a fossil fuel, coal forms from the decomposed, carbon-based bodies of plants and animals that lived and died a long time ago.

6:46 · And I’m talking before the dinosaurs.

6:48 · So no, I’m afraid that means you aren’t gassing up your car with the remains of a T-Rex.

6:53 · When we burn those fossil fuels— whether it’s in the form of coal, oil, or natural gas— carbon dioxide gets released into the atmosphere.

7:00 · When Arrhenius ran the numbers, he predicted that carbon dioxide released by burning fossil fuels could warm our climate within a few thousand years if we kept burning them at their current rate.

7:14 · But we’ve far outpaced his estimates.

7:16 · Our emissions of carbon dioxide have grown and grown and grown — and so has the mountain of evidence that those emissions are warming our planet.

7:25 · Some oil and gas companies have worked to promote uncertainty around the existence of climate change and what’s causing it.

7:32 · While they have only very recently acknowledged its existence, as of 2023, they’re still trying to deflect from what’s causing it.

7:40 · But the scientific consensus on this is overwhelming.

7:44 · You can learn much more about that in our Climate & Energy series.

7:47 · We’ve only been reliably taking direct measurements of Earth’s global temperature since the 19th century.

7:52 · But that measly slice of time shows a steady rise in temperature of about 1.1 degree Celsius since 1880.

8:01 · And we know that that’s unusual because we’ve learned to read Earth’s much longer climate diary, in the form of ice cores.

8:07 · See, when snow hardens into ice, tiny bubbles of air remain trapped in the gaps between snowflakes.

8:13 · These gases and water molecules stay frozen, like entries in a frosty journal.

8:19 · So by drilling deep down into polar ice, we can snoop on what the atmosphere was like hundreds of thousands of years ago.

Carbon Sinks

8:27 · Ice cores show us that carbon dioxide levels have fluctuated over the past 800,000 years.

8:32 · And temperatures have fallen and risen alongside them, too.

8:36 · But when people started burning fossil fuels, carbon dioxide levels began to spike quickly, like really, really quickly.

8:44 · And they haven’t stopped rising.

8:45 · Before the Industrial Revolution, for every million molecules of air in our atmosphere, around 280 were carbon dioxide molecules.

8:54 · But by 2022, that number had increased to 422 —the highest concentration of CO2 our planet has seen in 4 million years.

9:06 · And that surge in carbon dioxide affects more than just the temperature.

9:10 · As the amount of carbon dioxide in our atmosphere rises, it impacts all of Earth’s systems.

9:15 · Just like yanking on the end of that tangled mess of headphones impacted all of the other items in my bag (and eventually, everybody around us when stuff started falling out).

9:24 · It’s all connected, is what I’m saying.

9:26 · For example, the ocean is our planet’s largest carbon sink.

9:31 · It’s sort of like a big storage container for carbon.

9:34 · In fact, the ocean holds 50 times more carbon than the air or soil do.

9:40 · But a chemical reaction happens when carbon dioxide meets water, it creates an acid.

9:45 · So that influx of carbon has already turned the ocean 30% more acidic since the 19th century.

9:53 · As carbon dioxide and other greenhouse gases trap more heat, there’s more energy pouring into our planet than going out.

9:59 · That means more energy is pumped into the ocean, fueling hurricanes and typhoons to become more frequent and more intense.

Environmental Justice

10:07 · As the whole planet gets hotter, that triggers all kinds of changes.

10:11 · Spring arrives earlier, leading to shorter winters and longer summers.

10:15 · Rising temperatures lead to double-whammy droughts and heatwaves.

10:19 · And that leaves forest floors full of dried-up plants that serve as fuel for wildfires to ignite— spreading faster, farther, and more often.

10:28 · Plus, Earth’s polar ice caps are melting, transforming solid ice to slush and seawater.

10:34 · And that’s causing ocean levels to rise and encroach on land.

10:38 · So while some communities are already facing a problem of not enough water, others are facing a problem of too much.

10:45 · And because these adverse changes layer on top of existing social inequalities, they disproportionately affect lower-income communities and people of color, making climate change not only a scientific issue, but a matter of environmental justice that has spurred some researchers to political action.

11:02 · For example, climate scientist Nicole Hernandez Hammer witnessed first-hand, through her field research, the sea-level rise alongside Miami Beach’s Latino communities.

11:12 · But these communities were not included in conversations about climate change or the dangers of rising sea levels.

11:18 · So, Hammer took action, moving into environmental outreach and education.

11:23 · And, while the threat is still there, these communities are now in conversation about climate change, and able to plan and advocate for the future of their environment.

11:33 · And the good news more broadly is we know exactly why these tangled, complex effects are happening.

11:39 · The more we burn those fossil fuels, the more greenhouse gases we release —and that’s driving sweeping changes all over our planet.

11:47 · And these changes are impacting life at all levels from the tiniest bacterium, to the elephants of Botswana, to you and me.

11:55 · We can slow these impacts by breaking up with fossil fuels.

11:58 · But we also have to invest in nature’s carbon sinks —such as soils and forests— which pull carbon out of the atmosphere and back into the land, where it can’t keep heating things up.

Review & Credits

12:09 · It won’t be the easiest breakup.

12:12 · We’ve designed whole societies and global systems around fossil fuels.

12:16 · We should fully expect to be listening to Jazmine Sullivan on repeat with a gallon of Rocky Road.

12:21 · But this is one relationship we can’t afford to stay in.

12:24 · So, in order to halt emissions, we have to both invent new systems and work more efficiently within old ones, and to do that, we’ve got to get a whole planet’s worth of people on board.

12:35 · And if you’ve ever been part of a group project, you know that last part isn’t easy.

12:39 · Thankfully, when faced with global crises, humans have one great thing going for us: we are creative.

12:46 · I mean, we’ve been to space, we’ve got electric cars, we made furbies for some reason.

12:53 · So yes, It’s going to take all of our creativity and cooperation to tackle.

12:58 · But the only way out is through, and the only way through is together.

13:04 · In our next episode, we’ll tune back into the world of living things— and see how our rapidly changing climate involves much more than the atmosphere.

13:12 · It affects every living, breathing, organism on this planet— including you and me.

13:18 · This series was produced in collaboration with HHMI BioInteractive.

13:22 · If you’re an educator, visit BioInteractive.org/CrashCourse for classroom resources and professional development related to the topics covered in this course.

13:32 · Thanks for watching this episode of Crash Course Biology, which was filmed at our studio in Indianapolis, Indiana, and was made with the help of all these nice people.

13:41 · If you want to help keep Crash Course free for everyone, forever, you can join our community on Patreon.

What we know about toxic ‘forever chemicals’ and how to reduce our exposure

Video (6:21)
Description

A class of toxic chemicals known as PFAS has made its way into food, soil, water and even most people’s blood in America. In March, the EPA proposed the first regulatory standard limiting the quantity of PFAS in drinking water. Erin Bell, an environmental epidemiologist at the State University of New York at Albany, joins Ali Rogin to discuss these “forever chemicals” and our exposure to them.

Intro

0:01 · home of the pbs newshour, weeknights on pbs.

0:04 · John: There’s a class of toxic chemicals so pervasive that they are found in food, soil, water, and in the blood of most people in America.

0:13 · Earlier this month, the EPA proposed the first-ever regulatory standard to limit the quantity allowed in drinking water.

What are Forever Chemicals

0:20 · Ali rogin looks at the growing health concerns about these chemicals.

0:27 · Ali: Pfas, sometimes called forever chemicals, repel fire, water, oil and stains and have been used since the 1940s in a wide variety of everyday products.

0:35 · You can find them in nonstick cookware, fast food wrappers, clothes, and cosmetics.

0:42 · But these man made chemicals don’t break down easily, and a number of them have been linked to serious health problems.

0:47 · For more on their impact, we turn to Erin bell.

0:49 · She’s an environmental epidemiologist at the state university of New York at Albany who studies human exposure to these toxins.

0:55 · Erin, thank you so much for joining us.

0:57 · Just how widespread are these chemicals?

1:03 · Erin: These chemicals have been detected in the drinking water and soil in every state of the United States, and it is also being detected in many countries across the world.

1:13 · A number of health concerns are related to exposure to these chemicals.

1:18 · They include impact on the immune system.

1:21 · Folks with higher exposures are more likely to have thyroid changes, changes in kidney function, as well as higher cholesterol.

1:30 · We’re also concerned about impacts on children with regards to low birth weight and neurodevelopment in those children.

1:39 · And there are a number of other health outcomes where the literature is more mixed.

1:42 · We sometimes see an association, other times we see less of an association and we have more work to do.

1:49 · These would include infertility factors around ulcerative colitis and other autoimmune diseases.

1:55 · Ali: And are these things that people can determine by taking a blood test?

2:00 · I mean, how how does one test to see where their levels are?

2:05 · Erin: That’s correct.

2:07 · So for each individual, we measure concentrations in their blood, and that is the best way to determine the individual level of exposure for people who have lived in communities or for people in occupations that have these higher exposures.

2:17 · Unfortunately, there’s no consistent guidance or coverage from insurance companies for testing for pfas in your blood.

2:26 · If you contact your local health department, they will let you know if there are programs at the state level sponsored through state health departments, if they have opportunities for you to get tested.

2:37 · But this is a situation that we’re still working on in terms of making sure there’s coverage for people to get tested if they wish to know what their levels are.

EPA Proposal

2:48 · Ali: Now, the EPA, as we mentioned in the introduction, is proposing a new regulation pertaining to pfas in water.

2:54 · Can you explain what exactly this proposal would do?

3:01 · Erin: There are 12,000 chemicals in the family of pfas.

3:03 · The EPA has suggested that for six of these chemicals, there should be a standard federal rule that would require that water utilities test for these six chemicals.

3:16 · And if they were above a certain level, that those levels be monitored for and companies be required to reduce their exposure in the water utility.

Are we missing something

3:29 · Ali: Now six out of 12,000 doesn’t sound like a lot.

3:32 · Are we missing something here?

3:34 · Are these very significant versions of these chemicals?

3:37 · Erin: So that’s an excellent question.

3:38 · And this is one of the challenges we have in the field.

3:40 · Out of the 12,000, we’ve obviously studied very few of them.

3:45 · So these six are particularly of concern because we know the most about them.

3:51 · We also have detected them in higher levels in drinking water across the country.

3:56 · However, it is quite literally a drop in the bucket.

4:02 · It is just a very small number.

4:03 · There is concern in the scientific community that we will never catch up in terms of understanding what the health effects are for the much larger group of pfas’s.

How is it that these companies are able to continue

4:16 · Ali: How is it that these companies are able to continue to manufacture products with these dangerous chemicals in them?

4:25 · Erin: So historically, when we talk about environmental exposures and removing them from production, we focus on their persistence.

4:34 · They stay in the environment and in our bodies for a very long time.

4:38 · The newer versions are not as long lasting in the environment, however, because they are structurally designed to in essence do the same thing that made them good for production and for our consumer products.

4:49 · They’re still related to some of these adverse health outcomes.

4:51 · So they’re still being used because we have not regulated them and we still require them in the manufacturing process.

Reducing our exposure

4:56 · And until those rules and laws change, then that will be allowed to happen.

4:59 · Ali: So my understanding is that this EPA rule, if it is approved, is still going to take a few years before it’s fully operational.

5:05 · So what kind of things can people do if they want to begin reducing their exposure now?

5:08 · Erin: There are a number of things that people can do and communities can do.

5:17 · The first is awareness.

5:19 · Please be sure and keep mindful of what might be going on in your community, especially in the drinking water.

5:22 · If you are not sure what the levels of pfas are in your drinking water, you can contact your local water utility, your state health department, as well as any university or college researchers that are in the area.

5:39 · In terms of reduction, we can use filters on our water systems and in our homes.

5:42 · You also want to be mindful of what you use on an individual basis.

5:47 · Again, minimizing fast food wrappers, the wrappers are lined with these materials as well as microwave popcorn is another example.

5:53 · Pots and pans have these linings .

5:55 · As they become scratched the pfas can leach into the food and cause us more exposure.

6:04 · So again, be mindful of that.

6:07 · And as you replace them with things that are nonstick with that don’t have the nonstick chemicals or have the other types of coatings on them that would potentially have these chemicals.

6:14 · Ali: Erin bell with suny Albany, thank you so much for your time.

WHO Preventing disease through healthy environments

World Health Organization (WHO), 04 20, 2016

Video (2:00)
Description

An estimated 12.6 million people died as a result of living or working in an unhealthy environment in 2012 – nearly 1 in 4 of total global deaths, according to the World Health Organization. Environmental risk factors, such as air, water and soil pollution, chemical exposures, climate change, and ultraviolet radiation, contribute to more than 100 diseases and injuries. However, we can take actions to reverse the upward trend of environment-related diseases and improve our environments where we live and work.

Why decades-old, toxic PFAS foam is still contaminating northern Michigan waterways

Description

What is PFAS

2:03 · Practice putting out jet fuel fire is dowsing the burning planes with a special military foam used in the military since the 1970’s made from a chemical called p-fas. Courtney Carrigan is an environmental epidemiologist at Michigan state university who studies human exposure to chemicals.

2:22 · Characterized by a chain of carbons with fluorine bond and a functional group. One and likes water and the other does not, it repels it.

2:31 · The useful properties mean they are contained in all kinds of common products like nonstick pans, stain resistant carpet, and food packaging. There are about 15,000 types of p-fas and increasing the research shows they can cause health problems from liver damage to heart disease and cancer.

2:49 · They are often called forever chemicals because they are so persistent in the environment. They do not break down naturally. They move easily into water and groundwater and end up in fish and drinking water.

The scale of the problem

3:04 · Today about 50% of the nation’s drinking water supply has p-fas in it and nearly every American has some level of p-fas in their blood. The military is one of the biggest p-fas polluters in the U.S. But the problem is only now becoming known. In 2000 a colleague of mark Henry’s made the discovery and it sounded the alarm. Today the Pentagon confirms or suspects pfas concomitant – contamination at almost 700 other sites.

3:40 · There is almost no place you can dig a hole on the base and not find pfas in the groundwater even to this day 50 years after the release.

3:51 · The groundwater does not stay on the base.

3:55 · These plumes are flowing from the former base.

3:57 · Seeping for years into nearby streams, Lakes, and the tapwater of some homeowners like Tony.

4:03 · Right there.

4:04 · A politically connected attorney, he was so alarmed he teamed up to launch “Need our water” and call attention to the environmental Carla – calamity. They gave pbs a tour of the areas most affected by the pfas.

The plume

4:30 · This is a ymca camp owned by Detroit.

4:33 · The water is coming off the base from the storm sewers flowing uninterrupted into the saddle river.

4:39 · Tests found pfas levels more than 50 times higher than allowed by Michigan law. Today we visited dozens were swimming and boating nearby. About one mile away is a wildlife area called Clarks marsh where birds have been found with some of the highest levels of pfas contamination ever documented. There are five health advisory for the area warning to not eat any fish you catch or animals you hunt.

5:08 · This lake is highly contaminated on the surface. When the wind agitates the surface of the water the foam can build up.

Why managing chronic health conditions begins at home

PBS NewsHour, 12 07, 2018

Video (7:26)
Description

The recognition that health care extends beyond the walls of hospitals and doctor’s offices is growing. In Greensboro, North Carolina, advocacy groups, sociologists and medical practitioners are taking a creative approach to uncovering the root causes of disease, with a particular focus on poor housing conditions that can lead to chronic illness. John Yang reports.

3:35 · JOHN YANG: Sills’ students use the data to make referrals to the Greensboro Housing Coalition.

And so if you were to zoom way in on those, you’d be able to see these tiny scales, and those let the lizards cling to the smooth surfaces.

Shane: So for the Puerto Rican crested anole, what did you find?

One of my initial findings was that in the city, the lizards had longer limbs relative to their body length, larger toe pads with more of these specialized scales on the toe pads.

OK. Shane, voice-over: These are exactly the kinds of adaptations you might expect to see in cities, based on what we already know about anoles living elsewhere.

Kristin: They’re clinging to things like glass and metal and even painted concrete, which is a lot smoother than anything you find in the forest.

And so there’s kind of this combined selective pressure on toe pads, making them able to cling better, and limb length, making them able to navigate the structural environment better.

OK. Shane, voice-over: The urban lizards were different, but the real test was whether they could actually outperform their forest-dwelling counterparts when facing a uniquely urban challenge.

So I set up a lizard racetrack, and I fitted the racetrack with different surfaces.

One was tree bark, one was metal, like fence post metal, and then one was painted concrete.

And then I took high-speed video of these lizards racing up the racetracks.

And what I found was– or what I expected, rather, I should start there, is that the urban lizards would be able to run faster on the concrete and the metal and the urban substrates.

Those are the environments that they were living in.

Yeah, exactly.

And so they’d be better at doing that than the forest lizards.

What I found, though, was that the urban lizard’s just faster overall.

On anything.

On anything.

Even, like, bark and stuff that’s in forests.

Yeah, even bark, no matter what angle, no matter what structure we put on the track surface, they were faster.

They’re just hot rods.

Mm-hmm.

Shane, voice-over: In grad school, I studied how anoles in Texas– where the Polar Vortex causes rare but intense cold snaps– were adapting to the cold.

When I found out about Kristin’s work in cities, I realized her urban anoles could be facing a different kind of extreme.

Kristin: When I came down and tried out this big idea of “are anoles adapting to cities,” one of the things that I measured was body temperature and air temperature.

Shane, voice-over: Cities create what scientists call “urban heat islands,” areas with much hotter temperatures than the surrounding landscape.

So a few years ago, Kristin and I decided to find out how urban and forest anoles responded to extreme heat.

Sure enough, urban anoles could take the heat better than forest anoles, and we even found a few of the genes that affect heat tolerance.

These adaptations aren’t unique to any one city.

We found the same differences between urban and forest anoles again and again, all over Puerto Rico.

We can think of cities as the big Petri dishes that are replicated on a much larger scale.

Shane, voice-over: No two cities are exactly alike.

But for anoles, all cities have some things in common.

To survive on the streets, crested anoles evolved longer legs, bigger toe pads, and better heat tolerance than their forest relatives.

And it happened fast.

Some of our urban study locations have only been cities for 30 years.

That’s evolution in overdrive, even in lizard time.

So is this a feel-good story of animal adaptability?

Kristin: I think it’s a big mistake for us to think that all animals are gonna be able to adapt their way out of this.

We still are going to lose a lot of species as urbanization progresses.

OK. Shane, voice-over: Only two of Puerto Rico’s 10 anole species are found in cities, and just one, the crested anole, is really thriving.

So yes, some species can adapt, but the ones that don’t get squeezed out.

Kristin: I mean, that’s another reason why we should be studying the plants and animals that are doing well here, so that we can maybe engineer cities to be a little more favorable to wildlife.

Shane: So typically, when we think of ecosystems, right, we think of things like forests or deserts.

Should we consider cities as ecosystems as well?

Yes, absolutely.

And we should consider them as distinct ecosystems, just like we do all those others.

Shane, voice-over: Some folks aren’t as comfortable as Kristin with calling cities ecosystems.

But the more I learn about urban biology, the harder I find it to think of them as anything else.

Shane, voice-over: This is Peter Kahn.

When he isn’t serenading the wild on his homemade flute, he studies our species’ shifting relationship with nature.

Peter’s interest in cities dates back to the nineties, when he asked kids in Houston, Texas a couple of simple questions.

We just asked this kind of preliminary question, “Do you know what air pollution is?”

And they said, “Yeah, yeah,” all of them.

Then we asked the second question, which was, “Does Houston have air pollution?”

And the significant number of children went from, “Yes.

Yeah, I understand what air pollution is,” to “No, Houston doesn’t have air pollution,” which completely blew us away.

Because, at the time, Houston was the most polluted city in the United States.

How could these kids growing up in the most polluted city in the United States, how could they not know about that their city was polluted?

And that’s the start, for me, some 30 years ago, in thinking about this issue, about environmental generational amnesia.

That the children are coming of age, and they’re constructing a baseline of what normal is.

Shane, voice-over: That mouthful of a phrase, “environmental generational amnesia,” refers to the way that each generation grows up thinking that their experience of the world is normal.

So in our urbanizing world, the less nature we experience, the less we think we need.

With each generation, nature becomes smaller.

Wilderness becomes a city park and then a row of trees in a median and then…nothing at all.

And thanks to this so-called amnesia, most of us never notice.

Peter: I think we’re building urban prisons.

I think we’re imprisoning ourselves.

It reduces stress, reduces obesity, helps eyesight, reduces depression.

I mean, the list is on and on.

Yeah.

By centralizing the control of urban development, Singapore paves the way– you see what I did there– for projects that fuse function and form.

Cheong: If you look behind us, the Punggol Waterway is actually a man-made river.

We dammed up two rivers to become freshwater reservoirs.

Why don’t we make a river?”

So Punggol has a nickname.

Oh, that’s amazing.

Because we brought in water, right?

a very specific function.

Oh, that’s incredible.

Shane, voice-over: Singapore challenges a lot of what I thought I knew about cities.

No one’s saying that we should all be like Singapore, or even that we could be.

This place is unique.

90% of Singapore’s land is publicly owned.

The government determines what gets built and where.

And with everyone sharing the same tiny island, by definition, they’re all in this together.

Most places don’t have all that.

But I’m not sure we need it to re-imagine what cities can be.

I think the thing that stands out to me about Singapore is the intentionality.

I’m not sure if this specific model can be applied anywhere or everywhere, but I think they’re doing things that I think other cities can learn from.

We have to get our act together to make cities effective.

Creativity, innovation, and that will to survive and to want to do well are very, very strong drivers.

That’s human beings, right?

That’s what we are.

Shane, voice-over: Cities might just be humankind’s most remarkable invention.

Because we thought we were building ourselves a place to live, but we were really creating a new ecosystem, a place where nature has to adapt to live alongside us and some of our urban neighbors are more welcome than others.

City living brings challenges– for us and all the species that share our urban spaces.

But we shouldn’t forget that cities can also help us flourish.

In Oakland, Chris said something that stuck with me.

We are a communal species.

So for folks to think, “Well, if you just get outside the city, you’ll be OK,” nah, nah, nah, it doesn’t work like that.

We need to be able to have connection.

Shane, voice-over: That’s why cities aren’t going anywhere and why we need to make them better.

With a little creativity and vision, they can deliver that connection– to each other and maybe to the rest of our incredible planet, too.
